## [In-Context Superposition: LLMs Show Human-Like Working Memory Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09670) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new paper proposes that working memory limitations in large language models (LLMs) arise from representational compression and interference, a mechanism analogous to human cognitive constraints. The authors demonstrate this through experiments on a trained two-layer transformer and diverse LLMs, showing performance declines with memory load and retrieval biases similar to humans. This work bridges cognitive science and AI, offering a unified explanation for why LLMs, despite their vast capacity, exhibit human-like working memory limits. It could inform future model architectures and training methods to mitigate interference, potentially improving LLM performance on tasks requiring sustained attention to multiple items. The paper introduces the concept of 'in-context superposition,' where LLMs encode multiple memories in entangled representations. A causal intervention that suppresses interfering information improves retrieval performance, supporting the interference hypothesis. The study also finds that working memory performance in LLMs correlates with broader model capability, mirroring human findings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Working memory is the cognitive system that holds and manipulates information temporarily, and in humans it is limited to about three to four items. Large language models, despite having direct access to prior context via attention mechanisms, show similar limitations, which is surprising given their different substrate. This paper proposes that these limitations stem from a fundamental trade-off in shared representations: compression and reuse enable efficiency but cause interference among simultaneously active representations.

## [IACDM: A Structured Framework to Close the Verification Gap in AI-Assisted Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16399) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces IACDM, an 8-phase framework that uses external verification agents and adversarial critique to address the verification gap in AI-assisted software development. A pre-registered experiment with twelve projects found that all nineteen critique lenses were non-redundant, each identifying at least one unique defect. This framework directly tackles a critical, timely issue: the verification gap that causes experienced developers to be slower while believing they are faster, and leads to security failures like data leaks. By enforcing gates through an external state machine, IACDM could significantly improve the reliability and security of AI-assisted development practices. The framework consists of 8 phases (0–7), each with entry criteria, expected artifact, and an exit gate enforced by a state machine outside the generator model. The experiment showed that the taxonomy of nineteen lenses withstands testing, but the criterion for applying it does not, and the method creates commitments that are never re-confronted with the built system.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: The verification gap refers to the inability of language models to determine whether their generated code is correct without external tools. This issue has been highlighted in 2025 as a major failure pattern in AI-assisted development, where developers may be misled by internally consistent but externally wrong outputs. The DORA 2025 report also emphasizes that AI acts as an amplifier, magnifying existing organizational strengths and weaknesses, making structured verification processes even more critical.

## [Cat-DPO: Category-Adaptive Safety Alignment for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17299) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Cat-DPO introduces a novel direct preference optimization (DPO) algorithm that assigns a separate adaptive safety margin to each harm category, dynamically tightening or relaxing margins based on the model's current performance per category. This approach replaces the traditional uniform safety margin, leading to improved aggregate helpfulness and harmlessness across two LLM backbones and six preference-learning baselines. This work addresses a critical limitation in safety alignment, where models often appear safe on average but remain unsafe on minority harm categories. By compressing per-category safety variance and the best-to-worst gap, Cat-DPO offers a practical, drop-in refinement that can enhance the safety of LLMs without sacrificing helpfulness, potentially influencing future alignment strategies. Cat-DPO formulates safety alignment as a per-category constrained optimization problem, deriving dual margins that update dynamically for each harm category. Empirical evaluations on models like Alpaca-7B and Qwen3-4B show significant improvements in safe ratios and reduced per-category variance, effectively closing gaps in the worst-performing categories.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Direct preference optimization (DPO) is a popular method for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences by optimizing a policy directly from preference data. Traditional safety alignment methods apply a single, uniform safety margin across all preference pairs, which can lead to uneven safety across different harm categories. Cat-DPO builds on this by introducing category-adaptive margins, similar to earlier work like α-DPO that used adaptive reward margins, but specifically tailored for safety alignment.

## [Markov Decision Contests Prove Stationary Policies Suffice for Preference RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00367) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces a new problem setting called the Markov decision contest for reinforcement learning with pairwise preferences, proving that stationary Markov policies perform as well as history-dependent policies in long-horizon problems. It also shows that recovering an optimal policy exactly is in P and provides a simple iterative algorithm with sublinear convergence. This work bridges a significant gap between theory and practice in reinforcement learning with pairwise preferences, offering theoretical guarantees that were previously lacking. It could lead to more efficient and reliable alignment of AI systems with user preferences, especially in long-horizon tasks. The paper proves that stationary Markov policies are sufficient in the Markov decision contest setting, and that exact optimal policy recovery is in P. A deep-learning variant of the iterative algorithm is implemented and demonstrated to be efficient in long-term decision problems requiring function approximation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning (RL) typically uses scalar rewards, but pairwise preferences are often more natural for users to specify and can express goals that scalar rewards cannot. Markov decision processes (MDPs) are a standard model for sequential decision making, and stationary Markov policies are a class of policies that depend only on the current state, not on history. This paper introduces a new framework to address inefficiencies and lack of guarantees in preference-based RL for long horizons.

## [COPSD: Fixing Collapse in Safety-Oriented On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03089) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper identifies and formalizes the collapse problem in safety-oriented on-policy self-distillation (OPSD), attributing it to geometric leakage in a non-orthogonal semantic space, and introduces Constitutional On-Policy Safety Distillation (COPSD) to mitigate it. COPSD calibrates the teacher via a Cross-SFT cold-start and then performs constitution-conditioned on-policy distillation, improving both safety and helpfulness across 3 multimodal LLMs on 12 benchmarks. This work addresses a critical failure mode in AI safety alignment, where safety distillation can inadvertently reduce model expressiveness and helpfulness. By providing a theoretical framework and a practical solution, it has the potential to improve safe deployment of large language models, balancing safety with general capabilities. The paper formalizes geometric leakage as the transfer of safety pressure into the expressiveness dimension under safety boundaries in a non-orthogonal semantic space. COPSD uses a Cross-SFT cold-start to calibrate the teacher and then applies constitution-conditioned on-policy distillation, evaluated on 3 MLLMs across 12 safety and general benchmarks, showing reduced safety tax on general reasoning.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) is a post-training method where a model acts as both student and teacher, with the teacher conditioned on privileged information to provide dense token-level supervision. In safety alignment, the teacher is conditioned on high-level constitutions rather than explicit answers, but this can lead to collapse where the teacher distribution becomes overly conservative and less expressive. Reverse KL divergence, often used in distillation, can amplify this contraction. The proposed COPSD method aims to mitigate this by calibrating the teacher and using constitution-conditioned distillation.

## [Study Finds Q-K=V Projection Sharing Cuts KV Cache 50%](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04032) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A systematic study on arXiv (2606.04032) evaluates three QKV projection sharing variants in transformers, finding that Q-K=V sharing matches or slightly outperforms standard transformers while reducing KV cache by 50% with only 3.1% perplexity degradation. This finding offers a simple, effective way to reduce memory footprint during inference, which is crucial for deploying large language models on edge devices. It also deepens understanding of the roles of Q, K, and V projections, potentially guiding future architecture design. The study tested variants including Q-K=V, Q=K-V, and Q=K=V, and found that Q=K-V and Q=K=V produce symmetric attention maps, which they addressed with 2D positional encodings. Combining Q-K=V with GQA-4 achieves 87.5% cache reduction, and with MQA achieves 96.9%, enabling practical on-device inference.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Transformers rely on query, key, and value (QKV) projections to compute attention. KV cache stores keys and values during inference to avoid recomputation, but it grows with sequence length and model size, becoming a bottleneck for long-context and edge deployment. This study explores weight tying among Q, K, and V projections as a way to reduce cache size.

## [SkewAdam Cuts MoE Optimizer Memory by 97.4%](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19058) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers introduced SkewAdam, a tiered optimizer that reduces optimizer state memory in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) training from 50.6 GB to 1.29 GB, cutting peak training memory from 81.4 GB to 31.3 GB, enabling training on a 40 GB accelerator. In controlled tests, SkewAdam achieved better validation perplexity (108.4) than AdamW (126.8), Muon (120.2), and Lion (393.7). This work addresses a critical bottleneck in scaling MoE models, which are increasingly used in large language models. By drastically reducing memory footprint, it enables training larger models on existing hardware, potentially lowering costs and democratizing access to advanced AI training. SkewAdam allocates state based on parameter population: float32 momentum plus factored second moment for the dense backbone (5% of parameters), factored second moment alone for experts (95%), and exact second moment for the router (<0.01%). The memory savings come from the tiered allocation, not from improved accuracy; a tier ablation with twenty times more state achieved the same perplexity, indicating the tiers buy memory rather than accuracy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are a class of neural networks that use multiple specialized sub-networks (experts) to process different parts of the input, improving efficiency and capacity. Optimizer state, such as the first and second moments in AdamW, is stored during training to update model weights, and it can consume more memory than the model itself. Factored second moments, as used in Adafactor, reduce memory by approximating the second moment matrix as a product of two lower-rank vectors, cutting storage from O(nm) to O(n+m).

## [Strategic Oscillation: How Designers Vibe with Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23126) ⭐️ 8.0/10

An exploratory qualitative study of five expert designers (11-20 years UI/UX experience) reveals a new concept called 'Strategic Oscillation,' where designers intentionally alternate between vague and precise language when prompting generative AI. The study also proposes the ECRT cycle (Expectation-Collision-Reflection-Transformation) to model this process. This research provides novel insights into the micro-level cognitive processes of human-AI interaction in design, offering practical implications for AI tool design and design education. It positions AI as a 'probabilistic mirror' that can catalyze tacit knowledge externalization, potentially improving creative workflows. The study used think-aloud protocols and identified shifts from 'instruction' to 'consultation' mode, deep reflection triggered by semantic collisions, and one participant's disengagement as a tentative boundary condition. Three design patterns for metacognitive support were derived from the findings.

## [Moral Hazard in Multi-Agent Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23982) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces the Dialogue Moral Hazard Game, a theory-grounded experimental paradigm based on Holmström's model, to study moral hazard in multi-agent language models. It evaluates eleven open-weight and three frontier API models, revealing distinct behavioral patterns and showing that GPT-5.6 Sol tracks the Holmström-derived private-share boundary with high accuracy. This work bridges economic theory and AI alignment, providing a controlled framework to assess whether language models exhibit cooperative behavior in hidden-action settings. The findings highlight that optimization can improve aggregate rewards without restoring the intended cooperative mechanism, which is crucial for designing aligned multi-agent systems. The Dialogue Moral Hazard Game involves agents choosing between keeping an immediate local reward and paying a query cost to reveal a hidden safety fact that benefits another agent. The study decomposes behavior into query rate, realized information transfer, local-reward preservation, unsafe choice, format validity, and team success, and applies fine-tuning methods like SFT, RLOO, and GEPA to diagnose update mechanisms.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Moral hazard in teams, as modeled by Holmström, refers to situations where individuals in a group may shirk costly effort because the benefits are shared and effort is unobservable. The Dialogue Moral Hazard Game operationalizes this concept in a textual environment for language agents, allowing researchers to test whether models exhibit cooperative behavior. The study builds on economic theory to analyze AI alignment in multi-agent settings.

## [New CRoMa Metric Gauges Pathology Model Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25497) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces CRoMa (Cross-confounder Robustness Margin), a new metric to measure distributional robustness in pathology foundation models, addressing limitations of the existing Robustness Index (RI). It evaluates 20 tile-level and 4 slide-level encoders across multiple benchmarks, showing that higher CRoMa margins correlate with smaller performance drops after supervised adaptation. This metric provides a more principled way to assess and compare robustness across pathology foundation models, which is critical for reliable deployment in clinical settings where staining and scanning variations are common. It could guide model selection and training to improve generalization across institutions. CRoMa recasts robustness as a cohort-wide distribution of sample-level margins, measuring whether biologically matched samples with different confounders are closer than confounder-matched samples with different biology. The study found that all encoders contained confounder-dominated subsets, with prevalence and severity varying across models and cohorts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Pathology foundation models are large neural networks, often based on Vision Transformers, trained on histopathology images. They can encode non-biological variations from tissue preparation, staining, and scanning, leading to shortcut learning that undermines generalization across institutions. The Robustness Index (RI) was proposed to assess this, but it has structural limitations. CRoMa aims to provide a more reliable metric.

<a id="item-132"></a>
## [SE(3)-MeanFlow: Few-Step Protein Backbone Generation on Lie Groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27431) ⭐️ 8.0/10

SE(3)-MeanFlow introduces a few-step generative framework for protein backbone generation on the SE(3)^N manifold, extending MeanFlow from Euclidean space to Lie group geometry. It derives closed-form average-velocity identities for rotations and translations, and introduces an SE(3) alpha-Flow objective to avoid expensive ODE integration. This work addresses a major computational bottleneck in protein design, where existing diffusion and flow-matching models require hundreds of network evaluations for inference. By enabling few-step generation, SE(3)-MeanFlow could significantly accelerate high-throughput protein design campaigns, making it a valuable contribution to the field. The method works natively in the Lie algebra so(3) and R^3, deriving closed-form average-velocity identities for rotations and translations. It uses an SE(3) alpha-Flow objective as a warm-up stage, then switches to a small-t stabilized MeanFlow loss for the rest of pretraining and rectification-based post-training.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Protein backbone generation models often operate on SE(3)^N, the product of N rigid-body transformations, to represent the 3D structure of proteins. Diffusion and flow-matching models generate high-quality backbones but require solving an ODE over hundreds of steps, each involving a Lie group exponential map, which is computationally expensive. MeanFlow is a recent one-step generative model that regresses interval-averaged velocity, and SE(3)-MeanFlow adapts this idea to the Lie group setting.

## [I/O-Aware Reformulation Speeds Up Wavelet Convolutions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10805) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces an I/O-aware fused implementation of wavelet convolutions that reduces HBM traffic through three algebraic reformulations, achieving up to a 4.35x training speedup and roughly halving peak memory usage compared to the reference implementation. Wavelet convolutions are a promising drop-in replacement for standard convolutions, but their reference implementation is severely memory-bound. This work removes the systems overhead that limited their practical efficiency, making them more viable for deep learning applications. The reformulations include recomputing the Haar analysis butterfly on chip, collapsing the multi-level synthesis cascade into a single closed-form pass, and folding learned per-channel scales into convolution weights. The evaluation uses the WTConvNeXt configuration across decomposition levels and tensor shapes, showing a modeled HBM traffic reduction of approximately 2.55x.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Wavelet convolution (WTConv) expands a network's receptive field exponentially with decomposition levels while keeping parameter count linear. However, its reference implementation is memory-bound due to excessive data movement through high-bandwidth memory (HBM). I/O-aware algorithms, like FlashAttention, optimize memory access patterns to improve performance on modern hardware.

## [Verifiability Gap in Agentic AI Governance for FinTech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11344) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces the concept of a 'Verifiability Gap' in agentic AI governance for FinTech and proposes a multilevel governance theory, supported by empirical studies across nine model versions, from a 3-billion-parameter local model to a commercial frontier system. This work addresses a critical and timely issue in AI governance, highlighting that the binding constraint for agentic AI in FinTech is verifiability rather than capability. It provides a framework that could inform regulatory and audit practices in high-stakes domains, potentially shaping future governance standards. The empirical studies reveal that provider releases alter historical financial actions, and the frontier model rejects temperature, top_p, and top_k controls while exposing no random seed. Under the tightest controls, a local model reproduced 320 of 320 executions, while hosted models reproduced 319 of 320 and 959 of 960, and no execution record repeated in any configuration at any scale.

**Background**: Agentic AI systems autonomously plan, select tools, and execute multi-step tasks with little oversight, making governance challenging. The paper argues that the key issue is not capability but verifiability—the ability to explain and reproduce decisions. The proposed multilevel governance theory aims to address this gap, extending beyond finance to other high-stakes domains requiring auditability.

<a id="item-136"></a>
## [Presence Check Is Not a Safety Check: AI Guardrails Degrade Under Context Compaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11392) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper reveals that during a single-cycle context compaction, safety rules can degrade into residues that appear intact but fail to prevent prohibited actions, showing that presence-based audits give false assurance. The study documents all-case behavioral gaps of +34 and +57 points under two replay models, demonstrating that degraded residues lead to far more violations than intact rules. This finding is critical for AI safety and alignment, as long-running agents that rely on context compaction may silently lose safety constraints, leading to behavioral violations that are undetectable by simple textual audits. It challenges current evaluation practices and highlights the need for behavioral verification rather than mere presence checks. The study found that rule-form items are retained significantly more often than prominence-matched facts, which explains why presence-based checking feels adequate even though survival is not protection. Textual loss is regime-dependent: with a single rule, it follows a weld-or-drop pattern, while under a tighter budget, degraded predicate-loss residues appear; the hypothesized textual severing mode was not observed. Additionally, LLM-judge labels alone could have reversed conclusions, indicating evaluation pitfalls.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Long-running AI agents periodically compact their context by replacing the transcript with a model-generated summary to manage memory and compute. Previous work (Governance Decay; Chen, 2026) showed that dropping a standing safety constraint during compaction causes behavioral violations across many models. This paper builds on that by examining a single compaction cycle to understand how a safety rule is lost and what that implies for detection and evaluation.

## [RIFT: Rollout-Free World Action Models via Fixed Future Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11521) ⭐️ 8.0/10

RIFT proposes that world action models (WAMs) can reuse a fixed future K/V cache instead of iterative video rollout, reducing deployment latency. On LIBERO, RIFT achieves 98.8% success while cutting action-chunk latency by 68.2% to 89.1%. This finding challenges the assumption that iterative rollout is necessary for future-conditioned action generation, potentially enabling real-time deployment of WAMs in robotics. It could significantly reduce computational overhead and latency, making advanced world models more practical for physical systems. The paper shows that for Joint and Cosmos-2, replaying a fixed final-clean K/V cache nearly preserves execution, with end-effector displacement error of 1.7–1.9 cm and success rates of 97.9%–98.2%. RIFT uses learned anticipation tokens to construct the future cache in one backbone pass, retaining the original future-read interface.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: World action models (WAMs) are robotics AI models that jointly predict future world states and robot actions, often using video pretraining. Traditional WAMs rely on iterative video rollout to generate future representations, which increases latency. The key-value (K/V) cache is a transformer optimization that stores attention keys and values to avoid redundant computation.

## [Hamilton-Zero: Neural Tensor-Network Foundation Model for Quantum Ground States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11911) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Hamilton-Zero, a 0.5B-parameter neural tensor-network foundation model that learns ground states of arbitrary quadratic qubit Hamiltonians via manifold variational optimization, trained on hundreds of thousands of Hamiltonian systems and evaluated on systems up to 8100 qubits. This work demonstrates that quantum ground-state problems can be amortized across a universal set of Hamiltonians, potentially enabling practical quantum advantage for systems beyond classical simulation. It combines tensor networks, neural networks, and reinforcement learning, offering a new paradigm for quantum many-body physics and quantum computing. The model uses manifold variational optimization over centrally odd scalar functions on SU(2)^N, with Hamiltonian acting via Lie derivatives and custom automatic differentiation. It employs a novel SU(2) replica-exchange Langevin sampler and sharded natural-gradient optimization with an extended KFAC optimizer, trained on systems up to 64 qubits and fine-tuned up to 1024 qubits.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Quantum ground-state problems are central to quantum chemistry and materials science, but classical methods often fail for large systems. Tensor networks and neural networks have been used to approximate quantum states, but typically for specific Hamiltonians. This work proposes a foundation model that can generalize across many Hamiltonians, leveraging techniques from large language models and deep reinforcement learning.

<a id="item-139"></a>
## [Information Abundance Paradox: Long-Context Training Undermines Parametric Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12218) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces the 'Information Abundance Paradox', showing that training LLMs with longer contexts can reduce parametric knowledge encoding, leading to performance declines beyond an optimal context length. This is demonstrated in both pretraining and supervised fine-tuning. This challenges the common assumption that longer contexts always benefit LLM training, with significant implications for model training strategies and deployment. It suggests that scaling context length indefinitely may not be beneficial, even with abundant high-quality data. The paper finds an inverted-U performance curve in pretraining, where language modeling, NLU, and closed-book MCQA improve up to an intermediate optimum then decline. Mechanistically, informative context shifts gradient pressure from feed-forward networks to attention modules, increasing reliance on context during inference.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models are trained with long contexts to expose them to richer evidence, assuming this only helps. However, models can learn either by internalizing knowledge parametrically or by relying on context. This paper explores the trade-off between these modes, showing that abundant context can reduce the incentive to encode knowledge parametrically.

## [LLM-Assisted Multi-Agent Scheduling for Edge Stream Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12371) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces MAS-DecStream, a multi-agent scheduling framework that extends the Contract Net Protocol with LLM-assisted semantic negotiation for stream processing in mobile edge-cloud environments. It incorporates semantic CFP formulation, progressive context disclosure, multi-round proposal revision, negotiation memory, and deterministic validation. This work addresses the complex challenge of decentralized scheduling in heterogeneous edge-cloud infrastructures, where workload volatility and QoS constraints are critical. By integrating LLMs with the Contract Net Protocol, it demonstrates significant improvements in reducing latency violations and resource overcommitment, which could influence future scheduling systems in edge computing. Experiments using Alibaba ASI Trace show that MAS-DecStream reduces latency violations to 3%, eliminates resource overcommitment, achieves a conflict-resolution rate of 0.91 with 20 agents, and improves utility by up to 22% over a multi-round rule-based baseline. A separate 25-case evaluation reveals model- and prompt-dependent accuracy-cost trade-offs.

The Alibaba ASI Trace is a production GPU cluster trace used for research on scheduling and resource management.

## [PAC-Bayes Meta-LoRA Improves Cross-Domain LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12389) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA, a method that calibrates adaptation strength based on evidence quality for cross-domain few-shot personalization of LLMs. It decomposes personalization priors into user and domain components, achieving significant gains on benchmarks like HiCUPID. This work addresses overfitting and negative transfer in cross-domain personalization, which are critical for deploying LLMs in real-world applications where user data is sparse. The method's principled approach could improve personalized assistants and recommendation systems, making them more adaptive and reliable. The method uses a meta-learned LoRA initialization as both the adaptation start and prior center, adjusting update strength based on support-set size and predictive uncertainty. It also uses human-readable prompts for stable preferences and topology-preserving soft tokens for domain-specific conditioning, reducing cross-domain win-rate degradation by 47.9% and improving win rate by 110.2% under cold start.

PAC-Bayes provides a theoretical framework for generalization bounds, which helps regularize the adaptation process.

## [ε-MemEvo: Adaptive Cross-Task Memory Transfer for LLM Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12522) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces ε-MemEvo, a framework that enables cross-task knowledge transfer in LLM-based program evolution by storing task-agnostic tactic memories and using an adaptive injection gate to prevent negative transfer. On the GPT-5 backbone, it improves AUCC over AdaEvolve on all 8 tasks with a mean relative gain of +8.7% and improves early-stage convergence by +9.4% on average. This framework addresses a significant limitation of existing LLM program evolution systems like FunSearch and AlphaEvolve, which optimize each task in isolation and discard search experience. By enabling effective cross-task memory transfer, ε-MemEvo could accelerate automated algorithm discovery and improve efficiency across diverse optimization benchmarks, benefiting fields like mathematical optimization and systems engineering. ε-MemEvo stores prior experience as task-agnostic tactic memories—compact natural-language summaries of successful algorithmic strategies—rather than raw code, enabling transfer across tasks with different APIs and evaluators. The adaptive injection gate decides whether retrieved memories should be injected and at what intensity, and ablations show that naive memory injection can fail catastrophically while adaptive gating remains safe across all five ablation tasks. The gains incur less than 1% computational overhead.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-based program evolution systems like FunSearch and AlphaEvolve use large language models to iteratively evolve programs that solve specific tasks, often discovering novel algorithms. However, they typically treat each task in isolation, discarding search experience after completion. Cross-task memory transfer aims to reuse knowledge from previous tasks to improve efficiency on new tasks, but naive transfer can cause negative transfer if memories are semantically mismatched. ε-MemEvo addresses this by using task-agnostic tactic memories and an adaptive injection gate.

## [CAS: Causal Attribution Score for Local and Global XAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12555) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the Causal Attribution Score (CAS), a new architecture for causal explanation that allocates intervention effects using causal Shapley contributions and provides local and global summaries. In benchmark simulations, CAS achieved a mean Local CAS MAE of 0.107, outperforming one-at-a-time normalization (0.173) and global normalized absolute ATE vector (0.213). CAS addresses a critical gap in explainable AI by separating prediction from intervention effects, which is essential for decision-making in high-stakes domains. This could improve the reliability of feature attribution in causal inference and influence how XAI methods are evaluated and applied. The paper demonstrates that predictive SHAP/TreeSHAP rankings differ materially from Feature-CAS rankings on empirical datasets, e.g., in Pennsylvania, dep1 moved from predictive rank 13 to Feature-CAS rank 2. CAS is not a new Shapley formula but a local-to-global causal reporting layer with an explicit intervention target.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Predictive explanation methods like SHAP attribute model outputs but do not directly attribute intervention effects on real-world outcomes. Causal Shapley values extend standard Shapley values by using a structural causal model to account for cause-and-effect relationships, separating direct and indirect effects. CAS builds on these concepts to provide a causal attribution score that is coalition-aware and intervention-targeted.

## [Tighter Bounds for Multivalued Probabilities of Causation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12657) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper derives tighter bounds for multivalued probabilities of causation (PoCs) by incorporating causal information from covariates and mediators, extending prior work by Li and Pearl, and Shu et al. The proposed bounds are shown to be tighter than existing nonbinary bounds through simulations. This work advances causal inference by providing more precise partial identification of individual causal responses in multivalued settings, which is crucial for decision-making in fields like medicine and policy. Tighter bounds reduce uncertainty in attribution, enabling more reliable conclusions from observational data. The paper builds on Tian and Pearl's sharp bounds for binary PoCs (PN, PS, PNS) and Mueller et al.'s tightening using covariates and mediators. It extends these to multivalued settings, illustrating results with toy examples and validating via simulation studies.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Probabilities of causation (PoCs) quantify the probability that one event caused another, which cannot be directly observed and thus require partial identification. Partial identification methods provide bounds on causal quantities when point identification is impossible. Tian and Pearl derived sharp bounds for binary PoCs, and subsequent work extended these to multivalued settings. This paper further tightens those bounds by leveraging causal knowledge from covariates and mediators.

## [SEAG Framework Masks Sensitive Entities in RAG Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12675) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces SEAG (Sensitive Entity Alias Generator), a privacy-preserving framework that replaces sensitive entities in user queries and retrieved documents with aliases before sending them to external LLMs. Experimental results show that all SEAG models achieve over 80% accuracy on the User metric, and models like Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, and Phi-4 hide sensitive entities with total accuracies of 77.83%, 76.73%, and 74.91% respectively. This addresses a critical but often overlooked privacy gap in RAG systems, where external generators can access sensitive information in queries and retrieved documents. By enabling users to leverage powerful third-party LLMs without exposing confidential data, SEAG could enhance privacy compliance and trust in AI applications across industries. SEAG uses a lightweight model to locate sensitive entities, generate aliases, and construct an entity replacement table, which is then applied to both the user query and retrieved documents. The paper also constructs two datasets: one for fine-tuning SEAG models and another for evaluating the framework's overall performance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that improves LLM responses by retrieving relevant information from external knowledge bases. While existing privacy research in RAG focuses on preventing unauthorized access to sensitive data, the issue of external generators having access to confidential information in queries and retrieved documents is often overlooked. SEAG addresses this by masking sensitive entities before they reach the external LLM.

## [Spatial Memory Agent Enables Frozen VLMs to Self-Evolve Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12743) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Spatial Memory Agent (SMA), a framework that allows frozen Vision-Language Model (VLM) agents to improve spatial reasoning through parameter-update-free self-evolution, without relying on external spatial tools at inference time. SMA converts verified spatial experience into reusable lessons and uses a Transfer Reliability Score (TRS) to guide retrieval during deployment. This work offers a practical, parameter-update-free path for enhancing spatial intelligence in frozen VLM agents, which is significant for embodied AI and multimodal assistants where fine-tuning may be costly or infeasible. It demonstrates strong performance across multiple benchmarks and base models, potentially influencing future designs of self-evolving AI agents. SMA operates in a verifiable spatial environment, using verifier-guided reflection to distill compact transferable lessons from spatial experience. Each lesson is assigned a Transfer Reliability Score (TRS), initialized uniformly and calibrated from later retrieval outcomes; during deployment, lessons are retrieved via semantic filter and similarity-TRS combined ranking. The method achieves the highest macro average in every base-model block and the best accuracy in most of 20 evaluations across five benchmarks and four base VLMs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Spatial intelligence is crucial for embodied agents, robotic planning, and multimodal assistants. Existing approaches to improve spatial reasoning in VLM agents typically rely on post-training (e.g., supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning) or on calling external spatial tools (e.g., depth estimation, 3D reconstruction) during inference. SMA explores a complementary route: enabling a frozen VLM to self-evolve by accumulating and retrieving experience-grounded lessons at runtime, without parameter updates or external tools.

## [PROVE-RT: LLM Framework Generates Mechanized Proofs for Real-Time Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12762) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PROVE-RT is a new LLM-assisted framework that automatically generates PROSA/ROCQ theorem prover scripts for schedulability analysis in real-time systems. It uses dependency-aware informal sketches, retrieval from processed PROSA documentation, staged skeleton generation, and proof completion, achieving a 44.7% success rate on a curated evaluation set where direct prompting of state-of-the-art LLMs fails. This work addresses a significant gap in applying LLMs to mechanized theorem proving for real-time systems, potentially reducing the substantial manual proof-engineering effort required. It demonstrates that retrieval-guided and staged LLM assistance can improve automation in formal verification, which could accelerate certification of safety-critical real-time systems. The framework constructs a mechanization-oriented corpus from 1,191 real-time systems papers, containing 13,134 informal sketches with dependency information. The evaluation shows that PROVE-RT achieves a 44.7% success rate, whereas direct prompting of state-of-the-art LLMs fails to reliably generate valid PROSA mechanizations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Schedulability analysis is essential for certifying real-time systems, but traditional pen-and-paper proofs are hard to scale and validate. Mechanized verification using theorem provers like PROSA/ROCQ offers a rigorous alternative, but manually constructing such proofs requires deep domain expertise. Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in various tasks, but they lack PROSA-specific knowledge, making it difficult to generate correct scripts. PROVE-RT addresses this by guiding LLMs with dependency-aware sketches and retrieval from processed documentation.

## [ARAC-Bench: New Benchmark Evaluates Auto-Research Process Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12788) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ARAC-Bench, a new evaluation framework for Auto-Research systems, was introduced, shifting focus from final answers to the alignment and completeness of the research process. It benchmarks 11 state-of-the-art frameworks, with the best alignment score reaching only 67.9 out of 100. This benchmark addresses a critical gap in evaluating Auto-Research systems by measuring process quality rather than just outputs, providing a more meaningful assessment of their ability to mimic human research. It offers a scalable reward signal for training future autonomous research systems, potentially accelerating progress in AI-driven scientific discovery. ARAC-Bench uses two synergistic components: the Academic Cognition Skills system, which converts implicit reviewer expertise into stage-calibrated rubrics, and a three-stage diagnostic protocol that decomposes research into Proposal, Experiment, and Synthesis. Validation against Ph.D. candidate rankings shows a strong correlation of 0.8141, confirming its reliability.

## [CABS+ Enhances Model Merging with Adaptive Weight Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12842) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CABS+ is introduced as an extension to the CABS model merging framework, incorporating Adaptive Weight Allocation (AWA) and an asymmetric fitness function to optimize merging coefficients without gradient-based search. It achieves significant performance improvements over existing methods, with up to 16.97% better overall performance than AdaMerging and 12.93% better than WUDIMerging, while using less than 25% of the GPU memory and achieving nearly 4x speedup in merging time. This advancement addresses key scalability and performance bottlenecks in model merging, a technique crucial for building multi-task AI models without retraining. By reducing time complexity and improving robustness across diverse tasks and architectures, CABS+ could accelerate the adoption of model merging in real-world applications, benefiting researchers and practitioners who need efficient multi-task model integration. The paper evaluates CABS+ across 27 datasets and 5 models, including large language, small-scale language, and vision models, comparing it with CABS, AdaMerging, and WUDIMerging. The proposed Relative Synergy Score (RSS) quantifies model mergeability to guide model selection, and the asymmetric fitness function prevents high-performance tasks from dominating the optimization objective.

**Background**: Model merging is a technique that combines multiple fine-tuned neural networks into a single multi-task model without retraining, reducing storage and inference costs. ## [Query-Conditioned Reuse Boosts Long-Horizon Agent Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12847) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces query-conditioned reuse (QCR), a simple target-bound note that specifies how to reuse past trajectories, achieving 62.3% average success across WebArena, WorkArena, and AppWorld, which is 10.7 points above using full trajectories. This work identifies post-retrieval reuse as a distinct bottleneck in long-horizon agent memory, offering a simple yet effective method that improves performance while reducing token usage. It provides a framework to separate retrieval quality from the reuse problem, which could guide future agent memory designs. The evaluation framework holds candidate retrieval, target state, model, decoding, and tool budget fixed while varying the support delivered to the agent. QCR uses 48.9% fewer online tokens than full trajectories, and summary reranking selects a reusable memory for 94.8% of targets, placing end-task success within 1.8 points of an oracle selector.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Long-horizon agent tasks require agents to operate over extended periods, often relying on memory of past trajectories. Retrieval systems can find relevant past experiences, but how to effectively reuse them after changes in users, entities, or environment state remains challenging. Benchmarks like WebArena, WorkArena, and AppWorld provide realistic environments for evaluating autonomous web agents.

## [India's Consumer Protection Act 2019 Falls Short for AI Harms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12863) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new working paper (arXiv:2608.12863) analyzes whether India's Consumer Protection Act, 2019, adequately addresses AI-related harms and liability allocation across the AI value chain. It finds that while the Act's technology-agnostic definitions could apply to AI incidents, significant gaps remain in proving causation and mapping responsibilities. As AI systems become widespread in consumer applications, clear liability rules are crucial for consumer protection and innovation. This analysis highlights legal gaps that could leave Indian consumers without effective remedies and create uncertainty for AI developers and deployers. The paper notes that proving causation between AI defects and consumer harm is technically challenging because AI failures often stem from design choices rather than discrete defects. It also points out that the Act's framework assumes distinct roles for manufacturers, sellers, and service providers, but the AI value chain involves overlapping responsibilities among data providers, model developers, deployers, and users.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: The Consumer Protection Act, 2019, is India's primary legislation for product liability and consumer rights. AI systems, however, introduce unique challenges such as opacity, continuous learning, and multi-staged production chains, which traditional liability frameworks are not designed to handle. The paper is part of a broader global discussion on AI liability, with similar debates occurring in the EU and the US.

## [ReflectFact: Self-Reflective Agents Improve Multi-Hop Fact Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12877) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ReflectFact introduces a self-reflective multi-agent framework for multi-hop fact verification, featuring three key tasks: Explicit Reasoning Path Planning, Evidence-Drift Verification, and Reasoning Reflection Verification. It achieves state-of-the-art results on HOVER and EX-FEVER, outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.32% and 2.78% respectively. This work addresses critical limitations in existing multi-agent fact verification methods, such as insufficient global objective awareness and conflicts between parametric knowledge and evidence. By improving evidence-grounded reasoning, it enhances the reliability of automated fact-checking, which is crucial for combating misinformation on social media. The framework introduces three tasks: Explicit Reasoning Path Planning resolves implicit entities and decomposes claims into sub-questions; Evidence-Drift Verification re-answers by quoting evidence when the answer echoes parametric prior; Reasoning Reflection Verification re-examines and regenerates reasoning steps upon detecting inconsistencies. Experiments on HOVER and EX-FEVER demonstrate significant improvements over baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-hop fact verification is a task that verifies claims by reasoning over multiple pieces of evidence, which is essential for combating misinformation. Recent methods often use multi-agent collaboration to decompose the task into subtasks, but they may lack global objective awareness and can be misled by conflicts between parametric knowledge and evidence. Self-reflective agents integrate self-evaluation to adaptively enhance coordination and task performance, which is a growing trend in agentic AI.

## [Predictive Memory Localization: A New Framework for Activation Steering Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12892) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Predictive Memory Localization (PML), a novel evaluation framework for activation steering that treats the measured-grid intervention path as the predictive object of memory localization. It demonstrates modest but consistent improvements over random baselines across 3,000 records from nine datasets and fourteen domains, with the geometry-derived RFM/AGOP direction at layer 7 achieving 13.1% target-any and 12.3% clean-any, exceeding random by 3.6 and 3.4 percentage points. This work addresses a critical gap in activation steering research by providing a falsifiable and risk-aware evaluation framework, which could lead to more reliable and safer model control. It offers a systematic way to compare different localization methods and select intervention coefficients, potentially improving interpretability and AI safety practices. The study evaluates 30,000 distinct record-direction-layer paths and 210,000 distinct path-strength evaluations, using record-paired bootstrap for statistical significance. It finds that responses at |α|=0.1 are the strongest signal for outcomes at disjoint strengths |α|∈{0.25,0.5}, and a predictor-driven selector improves utility and reduces semantic-neighbor damage compared to a fixed-strength policy. Across three residual-norm-matched base models, low-dose responses yield 0.801-0.828 record-held-out macro AUROC.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Activation steering is a technique for controlling language model behavior by modifying internal activations during inference, often using steering vectors derived from contrastive examples. Mechanistic interpretability aims to understand internal representations, and methods like RFM (Recursive Feature Machine) and AGOP (Average Gradient Outer Product) are used to identify feature-relevant directions. PML builds on these concepts to evaluate whether a steering direction has a selective operating regime, separating random-calibrated target movement from semantic-neighbor and capability damage.

This work highlights a critical limitation in current vision-language models for medical applications, where visual grounding is essential. ## [DECAF Decomposes Perturbation Responses into Evidence, Contradiction, Fragility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12935) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces DECAF, a novel decomposition method that separates perturbation responses into evidence (E), contradiction (C), and fragility (F), preserving the exact magnitude (Abs = E + C + F) and unique under endpoint-relative axioms. It is validated across controlled vision and tabular settings, including a 72-model ImageNet-9 audit and a 1B-scale DINOv2 model. DECAF addresses a critical gap in perturbation-based explainable AI by interpreting response magnitudes, not just measuring them. It provides a more accurate and interpretable framework for model decisions, potentially improving trust and debugging in machine learning systems. In the ImageNet-9 audit, the largest DECAF component matched observed behavior in 96.4% of cases, compared to 35.0% for magnitude alone. Changing the reveal path increased total response by nearly 80%, with fragility growing over 4x while evidence barely changed. On a 1B-scale DINOv2 model, a short trajectory matched a strong gradient-based baseline with 4.75x lower wall time and 2.36x lower peak memory.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Perturbation methods in explainable AI measure how model predictions change under altered inputs, but the magnitude alone does not indicate whether the change supports or opposes the final decision. DECAF tracks the development of contrast as paired inputs are progressively revealed, using the final contrast to interpret the trajectory. This approach is grounded in the concept of factual-counterfactual differences, which are central to many explanation methods.

## [BoardroomAI: Human-Steerable Multi-Agent Deliberation with Evolving Decision Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13046) ⭐️ 7.0/10

BoardroomAI introduces a novel framework for multi-agent deliberation that treats humans as persistent participants, using typed decision graphs and an intervention compiler to enable real-time human steering. The framework achieves dependency-aware propagation that matches exhaustive impact computation while inspecting only 14.59% of nodes in experiments. This work addresses a significant gap in conventional transcript-based multi-agent systems, where human input is limited to initial problem definition. By enabling persistent human intervention, BoardroomAI could improve collaborative decision-making tools in organizational settings, making them more adaptive to evolving evidence and priorities. The framework comprises four components: a typed decision graph, an intervention compiler, dependency-aware propagation, and an evaluation framework. In a 12-case pilot, selective repair recomputed 62.11% of canonical nodes, preserved all gold-unaffected nodes, and produced valid decisions in six cases while abstaining in six, highlighting the need for decision-sufficient context closure.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent systems often operate in a black-box manner, where humans provide an initial problem and receive a final answer without ongoing input. BoardroomAI introduces a typed decision graph that captures evidence, assumptions, constraints, and dependencies, allowing for explicit representation of the decision process. The intervention compiler translates human actions into graph updates, and dependency-aware propagation selectively recomputes affected parts, improving efficiency. This approach is inspired by concepts like typed decision graphs used in business logic modeling and dependency-aware techniques in other domains.

## [DMDIntel: Interpreting LLMs via Dynamic Mode Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13048) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DMDIntel is a new method that uses dynamic mode decomposition (DMD) to interpret LLM predictions by attributing importance to input tokens. Experiments across three datasets and three model families show it outperforms existing techniques like PCA, integrated gradients, and SHAP. This work introduces a novel interpretability approach for LLMs, which is crucial for trust and safety in AI applications. By outperforming state-of-the-art attribution methods, DMDIntel could provide more reliable explanations, benefiting researchers and practitioners in model debugging and auditing. DMDIntel first decomposes the hidden states of an LLM into modes, then ranks input tokens based on projection values on those modes. The paper reports consistent improvements over PCA, integrated gradients, and SHAP, but the arXiv paper is a preprint and has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Dynamic mode decomposition (DMD) is a dimensionality reduction algorithm that extracts spatio-temporal patterns from time-series data, associating each mode with a frequency and growth/decay rate. Unlike PCA, DMD modes are not orthogonal but can capture dynamic behaviors. Input attribution methods like SHAP and integrated gradients aim to explain individual predictions by assigning importance scores to input features, which is essential for understanding and trusting LLM outputs.

## [VALG: Agentic System Automates ML Theory Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13060) ⭐️ 7.0/10

VALG is an open-source agentic system that automates machine learning theory research by combining multi-level verification, adaptive problem formulation, and graph-structured proof development. It was evaluated on nine subproblems from five COLT 2026 open problems, producing two theorem candidates that match the scope of their source briefs. This work represents a novel application of agentic AI to theoretical research, potentially accelerating the pace of discovery in machine learning theory. By automating problem formulation and proof development, it could shift how researchers approach open problems, making the process more efficient and scalable. VALG maintains a fixed mathematical specification within each source-relative theorem branch, checks theorem-level composition of a typed proof-dependency graph, and constructs and reviews local proofs in dependency order. When proof attempts fail, it identifies whether the obstruction lies in derivation, proof structure, or theorem formulation, and routes the next attempt accordingly, with formulation-level obstructions initiating explicitly related variants or relaxations.

**Background**: Machine learning theory studies learning procedures through mathematical setups where data models, training protocols, oracle access, loss, metrics, and randomness define the phenomenon a theorem explains. Agentic AI systems are autonomous AI systems that can plan, use tools, and adapt to accomplish tasks with limited supervision, as defined by IBM and others. Graph-structured proof development, such as development graphs, is a technique for managing structured specifications and reusing proofs in formal program development.

## [LLM Explanatory Engagement Varies with Anomaly Rarity and Elicitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13063) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This study investigates how LLMs' explanatory engagement changes as failure rates become asymptotically rare, finding that the expected rise in engagement is condition-dependent and not universal. Under immediate forced explanations, length and confidence increase with rarity, but plateau rather than collapse. This research challenges the assumption that LLMs uniformly increase explanatory engagement with rare failures, highlighting the role of elicitation structure. It has implications for designing reliable AI workflows and understanding model self-monitoring behaviors. The study used three open-weight models (qwen3:8b, llama3.1:8b, mistral:7b) in a tool-call task with failure probabilities from 0.2 to 0.0001, under five elicitation conditions. Notably, llama3.1:8b voluntarily provided structured confidence reports unprompted, while others did so only once as boilerplate.

## [Robust Dempster-Shafer Fusion with Chaos-Conflict and Historical Weighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13108) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper proposes a unified evidence reasoning framework that jointly quantifies cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence uncertainty via a chaos-conflict measurement, and uses a historical-experience weighting scheme based on spectral clustering and regret theory to weight evidence sources. The framework includes a hybrid combination rule and a belief-interval decision strategy, achieving an average F1 of 85.78 and mean AUC of 93.30 on 16 benchmark datasets. This work addresses two persistent limitations in Dempster-Shafer evidence fusion: independent conflict and uncertainty measures, and reliance on instantaneous comparisons without considering long-term reliability. The proposed framework improves fusion robustness and accuracy, potentially benefiting multi-source decision-making applications in fields like sensor fusion, pattern recognition, and medical diagnosis. The chaos-conflict measurement has five formally proven properties ensuring consistent assessment. The historical-experience weighting partitions the decision space via spectral clustering and applies regret theory to compute context-specific reliability profiles. The hybrid combination rule adaptively balances uncertainty preservation against weighted consensus, controlled by the global conflict level, followed by a belief-interval decision strategy that preserves epistemic uncertainty.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Dempster-Shafer theory (DST) is a general framework for reasoning with uncertainty, allowing combination of evidence from different sources. Traditional conflict measures assess inter-evidence inconsistency and intra-evidence uncertainty independently, which can be incomplete. Spectral clustering uses eigenvalues of a similarity matrix for dimensionality reduction before clustering, and regret theory models decision-making under uncertainty by incorporating anticipated regret. This paper integrates these concepts to improve evidence fusion.

## [SkillEvo: Self-Renewing Evolution Gradients from Multi-Turn Interaction Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13120) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SkillEvo introduces a novel method for agent skill evolution that uses multi-turn interaction feedback to generate self-renewing evolution gradients, overcoming the limitations of single-turn evaluation. It surpasses self-reflection-based evolution by 23.0 points and single-turn-QA-driven evolution by 15.4 points across six categories of cloud services, 9 production Skills, and 98 skill-reference files. This work addresses a critical bottleneck in AI agent skill improvement: the decay of evolution gradients after initial single-turn fixes. By enabling sustained evolution through multi-turn feedback, SkillEvo could significantly enhance the adaptability and long-term performance of AI agents in real-world applications, particularly in complex service domains. SkillEvo consists of two main components: a feedback generator that recasts multi-turn user simulation to expose defects layer by layer, and an independent governance layer that actively repairs factual degradation and structural bloat. The method uses an intent state machine, dual-sided orthogonal evaluation, and independent attribution screens to ensure trustworthy feedback and controlled evolution direction.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Agent skills are typically hand-authored or generated in a single LLM pass, lacking a closed loop for improvement from interaction failures. Recent work attempts to close this loop but relies on single-turn question-answering evaluation, which leads to a sharp asymmetry: after the first round patches gaps visible in a single exchange, the evolution gradient decays and multi-turn defects remain invisible. SkillEvo addresses this by using multi-turn interaction feedback to generate continuous evolution gradients, drawing on concepts from reinforcement learning and evolutionary algorithms.

## [Post-Norm Beats Pre-Norm in Curriculum Depth Growing for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13156) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper demonstrates that post-normalization outperforms pre-normalization in Transformers when depth is introduced via curriculum growth, contrary to standard practice. In a controlled distillation study with a Qwen3-8B teacher and a nine-layer student, post-norm improves validation cross-entropy by 0.0328 over pre-norm under curriculum growth, while they are indistinguishable under joint training. This finding challenges the conventional wisdom that pre-norm is always preferable in modern Transformers, suggesting that normalization placement and training curriculum should be treated as coupled design choices. It could influence how future LLMs are trained, especially in progressive or curriculum-based depth scaling approaches. The study uses a controlled distillation setup with a Qwen3-8B teacher and a nine-layer student, and includes controls such as post-joint training matched by student active-layer tokens, single-block, and freeze controls. The ranking crossover occurs during the curriculum, with post-norm taking the lead once blocks are appended, and boundary diagnostics associate post-norm with stable residual scales and pre-norm with structural-token scale drift.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Pre-norm (pre-layer normalization) is the standard placement in modern Transformers because it facilitates joint optimization of full-depth models, while post-norm (post-layer normalization) is often harder to train. Curriculum depth growth is a training strategy where model depth is increased progressively, and each appended block receives the boundary representation from the trained prefix, making normalization placement relevant to forward conditioning. This paper explores the interaction between normalization placement and curriculum depth growing in a distillation setting.

## [SkillShapley: Boundary-Adaptive Shapley for Step Attribution in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13173) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SkillShapley introduces a boundary-adaptive Shapley value framework to quantify the contribution of individual steps within LLM agent skills. It operates in two phases, first identifying informative coalitional regions and then adaptively sampling new coalitions to yield reusable marginal evidence. This addresses the open problem of step-level attribution in LLM agent skills, which is crucial for debugging and improving agent performance. The framework could enable more efficient and effective skill creation and optimization, impacting the broader AI/ML research and application ecosystem. SkillShapley leverages empirical insights that discretized benchmark rewards create sharp performance cliffs and that step interactions are largely additive rather than synergistic. Experiments on SkillsBench demonstrate its effectiveness and efficiency in identifying high- or low-value skill steps.

The paper introduces CrEST, a hierarchical credit assignment framework that combines verifier-bounded reinforcement learning with dense token-level signals from a self-teacher. Experiments on BFCL V3 and WildToolBench show consistent improvements over RL and distillation baselines across two model scales. This addresses a known limitation in RLVR for multi-turn tool-use agents, where trajectory-level credit assignment conflates per-turn outcomes. By enabling dense credit assignment without sacrificing the verifier-bounded ceiling, CrEST could improve training efficiency and performance for complex agent tasks. CrEST resolves credit at two levels: turn-segmented verified advantages address inter-turn dilution, while entropy-gated self-teacher modulation refines intra-turn token contributions. The largest gains are observed on long-trajectory and strict session-level metrics.

## [TsuGO Benchmark Evaluates LLM Search Efficiency via Go Life-and-Death Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13221) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TsuGO is a new process-level reasoning benchmark that uses Go life-and-death problems to evaluate search efficiency in LLM reasoning. It parses chain-of-thought into a structured search tree and reports metrics such as Search Efficiency and Token Efficiency. This benchmark addresses a gap in LLM evaluation by measuring how models organize search and allocate reasoning resources, beyond just final-answer accuracy. It provides a structured, verifiable environment that could lead to better understanding and improvement of LLM reasoning capabilities. Experiments show that current LLMs are far from stable tsumego solving; stronger models find correct candidates earlier and sustain effort on productive branches, but most behave like unguided search algorithms. Longer chain-of-thought or higher token efficiency does not necessarily imply better search.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Go life-and-death problems (tsumego) are puzzles that require determining whether a group of stones can be made alive or killed, offering closed and verifiable solution spaces with adversarial structure. Process-level evaluation of LLM reasoning focuses on how models plan and execute reasoning steps, rather than just the final answer. TsuGO leverages these problems to disentangle domain knowledge from search organization, providing a novel way to assess search efficiency.

## [StateBridge: Training-Free Hidden-State Alignment for Latent Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13317) ⭐️ 7.0/10

StateBridge proposes a training-free latent communication method that aligns the sender's final-layer hidden states to the receiver's input space via a closed-form orthogonal transformation, avoiding the information loss of text tokenization. It achieves the best or tied-best score on 22 out of 26 model-task pairs across math reasoning, code generation, and question answering. This work addresses a key bottleneck in LLM multi-agent systems—text-based communication loses information when converting continuous hidden states to discrete tokens. By enabling training-free latent communication, StateBridge could improve collaboration efficiency and portability across different models, potentially advancing multi-agent reasoning and deployment. The method uses a closed-form orthogonal transformation, lightweight norm calibration, and vocabulary anchoring to ensure compatibility with the pretrained input distribution. The aligned states are prepended as a continuous prefix to the receiver's input, and the approach is evaluated on four models from two families.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: LLM multi-agent systems typically communicate via text tokens, which introduces a discrete bottleneck that loses information from continuous hidden states. Latent communication, where agents transmit hidden representations directly, has been explored but often requires trained projectors or layer-by-layer injection, limiting portability. StateBridge offers a training-free alternative by aligning hidden states with a closed-form transformation, making it more portable and efficient.

The Decomposer module builds on decomposition techniques that break complex tasks into subtasks, and YAML configuration allows non-technical users to customize agents without code.

## [Updating AI Accountability for the LLM Era](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12320) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper proposes three interlinked updates to the AI accountability ecosystem framework to address challenges posed by large language models (LLMs), including reorienting to AI infrastructure and supply chains, emphasizing outcomes monitoring, and incorporating end-user accountability. This update is significant because it shifts AI accountability from a discrete product model to a distributed, continuous, and institutionalized approach, which is crucial for governing LLMs that are unpredictable in-the-wild and involve complex supply chains. It provides a timely framework for policymakers and researchers to address emerging governance gaps. The three proposed updates are: (i) reorienting the accountability ecosystem to AI infrastructure and supply chains, (ii) providing greater emphasis on outcomes monitoring and identification of issues that support decentralized system improvement, and (iii) incorporating end-user accountability given the new risks of unpredictability of language models in-the-wild. The paper builds on the original AI accountability ecosystem framework, which predated the generative AI revolution.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: The AI accountability ecosystem framework was originally developed to ensure responsible AI development and deployment by mapping roles and responsibilities across stakeholders. With the public release of large language models like ChatGPT, the AI landscape has shifted from discrete products to complex, distributed systems involving multiple actors and infrastructures, making traditional accountability models insufficient. This paper updates the framework to reflect these changes.

## [LLM Self-Reflection Gains Driven by Action Routing, Not Vocabulary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12322) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A controlled six-condition ablation study on LLM self-reflection in armed conflict forecasting found that structured diagnostic questions and taxonomy vocabulary add no measurable value, while typed action routing provides consistent gains (F1: 0.379 vs 0.296). This study challenges the common assumption that self-reflection improves LLM reasoning, pinpointing action routing as the key mechanism. It could guide future design of metacognitive forecasting agents and influence research on LLM reasoning components. The ablation isolated four components: evidence exposure, diagnostic scaffolding, taxonomy vocabulary, and action routing. Null results for diagnostic scaffolding (F1=0.296 vs 0.297, p=1.000) and taxonomy vocabulary (ΔF1=+0.008) were replicated on GPT-4o, while action routing showed significant gains (p=0.025).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: LLM self-reflection is a technique where a model reviews its own reasoning to improve accuracy, but its effectiveness is debated. This study uses a controlled ablation to isolate which components of self-reflection actually contribute to performance gains in forecasting tasks.

## [Nepali ASR Benchmark: Whisper Turbo and IndicWav2Vec Tie](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12327) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new study fine-tuned six multilingual pre-trained models on the OpenSLR SLR54 Nepali corpus and found that Whisper-Large-v3-Turbo and IndicWav2Vec achieve nearly identical WER (14.76% vs 14.89%), despite a 9x parameter gap and 40x pretraining-data gap. This is the first standardized, efficiency-aware benchmark for Nepali ASR. This finding provides empirical evidence that language-family proximity in pretraining can substitute for raw scale for in-domain ASR, which is crucial for low-resource languages. It also shows that CTC decoders run up to 29x faster than autoregressive Whisper at similar accuracy, potentially shifting deployment preferences toward CTC models. The study evaluated WER, CER, and RTF on three independent test sets (OpenSLR, FLEURS, Common Voice) using identical preprocessing, splits, optimizer, and family-matched learning-rate schedules. Massively multilingual pretraining (MMS-1B) showed the smallest out-of-domain degradation on FLEURS (+12.55 pp), indicating scale buys robustness rather than peak in-domain accuracy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Multilingual pre-trained models like Whisper and XLSR-53 are designed to support many languages, but their performance on low-resource languages like Nepali is often untested. This study provides a controlled comparison by fine-tuning six models under identical conditions, offering a reliable reference for practitioners. The models span different architectures: CTC self-supervised (XLSR-53, IndicWav2Vec), autoregressive encoder-decoder (Whisper variants), and hybrid Conformer-CTC.

## [AnchorSIPS: Synthetic Dataset for Psychosis-Risk Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12329) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AnchorSIPS introduces a synthetic dataset of 10,000 structured psychosis-risk interviews, each modeled on the Mini-SIPS interview, with transcript-grounded labels for every intermediate decision and the final APS diagnosis. The dataset is generated via a plan-then-realize pipeline that fixes labels and structure before an LLM generates patient utterances, avoiding inter-turn inconsistencies. This dataset addresses a critical data-access bottleneck in AI for psychosis-risk assessment, where real clinical interviews are difficult to share due to privacy and consent constraints. By providing a privacy-preserving, synthetic resource with structured labels, it enables research on evidence extraction, transcript-grounded measurement, and uncertainty under partial disclosure, potentially accelerating progress in mental health AI. The dataset captures history, 24 symptom questions, follow-up evidence, decisions about delusion-like, hallucination-like, and disorganized communication symptoms, exclusion of frank psychosis, and the final APS diagnosis. Across seven LLM baselines, models recover coarse decisions but fail to extract follow-up details or cite supporting transcript turns, indicating that final-label performance overstates interview competence.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: Attenuated Psychosis Syndrome (APS) is a proposed diagnosis in DSM-5 Section III, characterized by psychotic-like symptoms that are below the threshold for a full psychotic disorder. The Mini-SIPS is a condensed version of the Structured Interview for Psychosis-Risk Syndromes (SIPS), designed to efficiently identify the minimum information needed to support an APS diagnosis. Synthetic data generation in healthcare has emerged as a solution to data scarcity and privacy concerns, enabling AI research on sensitive medical data.

## [TAM: Thought-Aware KV Cache Compaction for Reasoning LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12331) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces Thought-Aware Attention Matching (TAM), a novel method that compresses KV caches in reasoning language models by exploiting the hierarchical structure of chain-of-thought sequences. TAM achieves up to 65% peak memory reduction on AIME 2024 and MATH-500 benchmarks with Qwen3-4B while maintaining competitive accuracy. KV cache growth is a major bottleneck for long chain-of-thought reasoning in LLMs, limiting their practical deployment. TAM's adaptive, structure-aware compression offers a significant improvement over uniform methods, potentially enabling longer reasoning and lower memory costs for real-world applications. TAM consists of three mechanisms: thought segmentation, adaptive budget allocation, and pivotal token protection. The paper provides theoretical proofs that the allocation rule is optimal under a convex error model and that cumulative error remains bounded, with experiments on AIME 2024 and MATH-500 using Qwen3-4B.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 14, 04:00

**Background**: KV cache stores key and value vectors for previously generated tokens, avoiding recomputation during decoding, but it grows linearly with sequence length. Chain-of-thought reasoning generates long sequences, making KV cache a memory bottleneck. Existing compression methods treat tokens uniformly, ignoring the varying importance of different reasoning steps.
